Preface 

This report presents a record of the status of foreign debts owed to the U.S. Government and to international organizations, as of December 31, 1969. It has been compiled by the Department of the Treasury pursuant to section 634(f) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended (Public 

Law 89-583), which states:

The Secretary of the Treasury shall transmit to the Speaker of the House of Representatives and to the 

Committee on Foreign Relations of the Senate semi annual reports showing as of June 30 and December 31 of each year the repayment status of each loan theretofore made under authority of this Act any part of the principal or interest of which remains unpaid on the date of the report.

Part I of the report lists by country and by program active foreign loans and credits extended by the U.S. Government between July 1, 1941 and December 31, 1969. An active credit is one with an outstanding principal indebtedness or an available unused authorization. minor exceptions (see Explanatory Note), fully repaid loans and credit have been excluded.

In addition to the detailed listings for each country, tables are also provided which summarize outstanding credits according to area, country, program and currency of repayment. 

The loans and credits tabulated in Part I have been authorized under the following legislation, as amended: (a) Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, legislation for predecessor programs, and the Foreign Military Sales Act 1/ (b) (c) (d) 

Mutual Educational and Cultural Exchange Act of 1961 Agricultural Trade Development and Assistance Act of 1954 

(Public Law 83-480) Commodity Credit Corporation Charter Act (credits extended since 

April 27, 1967) 1/ All military sales, beginning with fiscal year 1969 have been extended under the Foreign Military Sales Act. Previous foreign military sales were extended under the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961. 1217730

(e) (f) (g) (h) (i) (j) (k) 

Export-Import Bank Act of 1945 Lend-Lease Act of 1941 Surplus Property Act of 1944 and related legislation Merchant Ship Sales Act of 1946 Atomic Energy Act of 1954 Euratom Cooperation Act of 1958 

Special legislation enacted for particular transactions, such as the British loan of 1945 and the loans to the United Nations. 

Part I also contains supplementary tables showing the arrearages of countries for which principal and/or interest is past due by 90 days or more as of the date of this report, and debts owed the U.S. Government as a result of World War I (including the amounts due from Germany). 

Part II shows the active loans and credits extended by the international development-lending institutions to which the U.S. Government has made capital contributions.

The Department of the Treasury expresses its appreciation for the cooperation of the U.S. agencies engaged in foreign lending and the international institutions in making the requisite data available for this report.

Part I Foreign Debts Owed to the United States Government

Part I-Explanatory Note SOURCE OF DATA 

Data on active World War II and postwar credits were compiled from reports submitted to the Department of the Treasury by the various operating agencies as prescribed by Treasury Circular No. 1080 and Treasury

Fiscal Requirements Manual, Part II, Chapter 4500. 

EXPLANATION OF TABLES Credit Program 

Titles listed under "Credit Program" indicate the major programs under which foreign indebtedness to the U. S. Government has been created. These programs have been undertaken pursuant to the various legislative acts listed in the Preface. 

In this report credits under the Export-Import Bank consolidate amounts held by the Bank for its own account and amounts of participations in loans sold to Secretary of the Treasury Deposit Accounts related to foreign government defense procurement in the United States. On December 31, 1969 the principal sum outstanding of such participations aggregated $204.6 million. 

Transactions reported as "Currency loans to foreign governments" under the Agricultural Trade Development and Assistance Act of 1954, as amended 1/ 

are primarily to foreign central governments. However, approved borrowers in this category have also included private entities, educational institutions, technical institutes, local governments, and development banks. 

This subclassification includes credits arising from disbursements of foreign currencies under the following subsections of the Agricultural Trade 

Development and Assistance Act of 1954, as amended: 

17 The Agricultural Trade Development and Assistance Act of 1954 (Public 

Law 83-480) has been amended many times. The citations in this text refer to the subsections as now in effect. 3

(a) (b) (c) 104 (c), "to provide equipment, materials, facilities, and services for the common defense." 

104(f), "to promote multilateral trade and agricultural and other economic development." 2/ 

104(g), whereby the U. 5. Government provides to one country the currency of a second country for purchase of goods and services from the country whose currency is disbursed. The country to which the currency is provided assumes the obligation to repay the U. S. Government. 

Borrower and Purpose

Entries in the "Borrower" and "Purpose" columns were provided for this report in many instances by automatic data processing methods from the operating agencies and have been reviewed or edited by the Department of the Treasury only to a limited extent. 

Under the Foreign Assistance Act in the subclassification "Country loans" - the Agency for International Development (AID) makes some two-step loans. In these loans, the host foreign government may elect to collect the repayments from the borrower (or primary obligor) and then repay the U. S. 

Government on an extended schedule. In some instances the borrower repays the host government in its local currency at an agreed interest rate, which is generally higher than the rate of interest repaid to the U. S. Government by the host government. While this report shows as the borrowers the entities actually receiving the loan or credit, the interest rates shown for such credits are those the host governments would pay in the event they elect, as they generally do, to collect from the borrowers and repay the U. S. Government. Principal and interest collections recorded are those actually received by the U. S. Government, whether from the primary obligor or the foreign government. 

2/ This section of the Act, under which the majority of the loans of foreign currencies has been made, was formerly subsection 104(g).

Date Authorized 

Entries in the "Date Authorized" column are generally those of original authorization or commitment, and do not reflect subsequent amendments. 

Dates which are known not to represent the date of authorization bear the following reference symbols: 

L Date shown is that of enactment of enabling legislation. 

R Date shown is the date of signature of the loan agreement with the borrower, as the date of authorization was not reported by the operating agency.

Date shown is that of the original credit sales contract.

T Date shown is that published in Treaties and Other International 

Acts Series (TIAS). 

Amount Authorized 

Entries in the "Amount Authorized" column are shown net of cancellation, expiration, or termination of any part of the original commitment. Portions of loans that have been sold or transferred to a participating lender such as a commercial bank or other financial institution are also deducted from entries in this column. Entries in the "Status of Credit" columns also exclude activity on portions of credits sold or transferred. 

The difference between the figure shown for "Amount Authorized" and the amount "Utilized" (or disbursed) represents the amount that, as of the date of the report, was available for further utilization. 5

Interest and Commissions Collected

Entries in the "Interest and Commissions Collected" column include, in addition to interest on outstanding indebtedness, regular collections for service charges, commitment fees on undisbursed balances, and the commission which the Export-Import Bank collects when a private institution participates in a loan of the Bank.

Interest Rate 

In the "Interest Rate" column, the following symbols are used for certain loans with relatively complicated rate provisions: 

A Borrower has option to pay interest and principal in either U. S. dollars or foreign currency: 3% interest if paid in U. S. dollars, and 

4% interest if paid in foreign currency.

B Borrower has option to pay interest and principal in either U. S. dollars or foreign currency: 4% interest if paid in U. S. dollars, and 

5% interest if paid in foreign currency.

C Interest rate on amounts used for agricultural projects is 

5.25% and on all other amounts is 3.5%.

D Borrower has option to pay interest and principal in either U. S. dollars or foreign currency: 38 interest if paid in U. S. dollars at weighted average exchange rate of deposit, 4% interest if paid in U. S. dollars at mutually agreed rate, and 5% interest if paid in foreign currency.

E Estimated average rate. This symbol appears where different rates of interest are applicable to parts of a credit. 

The average interest rate is weighted according to individual rates applicable to particular disbursements or to suballocations of the authorization. 

F Rate shown includes 0.75% service charge, payable in U. S. dollars; the remainder is interest, payable in borrower's currency. 

G Rate shown applies after grace period; rate during grace period is 0.75%.

H Rate shown applies after grace period; rate during grace period is 1%.

J Rate shown is that established in latest contract amendment; some outstanding balances on earlier deliveries are subject to interest at 0.75%. 

K Interest rate is to be determined.

Rate shown applies after grace period; rate during the grace period is 2%. 

W Interest free.

Z Interest free. Some interest was collected before the loan was amended to an interest-free status.

FOREIGN CURRENCY TRANSACTIONS 

In addition to loans and credits repayable in dollars, this report also lists transactions which are denominated in and/or repayable in foreign currencies. Such transactions have occurred primarily under the various subsections of the Agricultural Trade Development and Assistance Act of 1954, as amended, whereby the U. S. Government has sold surplus agricultural commodities in many countries and has received foreign currencies in payment, part of which may then be relent by the United States for economic development and other purposes specified in the Act. Foreign currency transactions have also been generated under predecessor programs to the present Foreign Assistance Act. 

Currency of Repayment 

Exchange Risk 

Under each program, each loan or credit appears under one of four categories, according to the currency in which the principal amount is to be repaid. The phrases "without/with maintenance of dollar value" are employed to indicate whether or not the United States assumes an exchange risk on the principal amount outstanding when repayment may be made in foreign currency at the option of the borrower. (a) "Repayable in U. S. dollars." This includes loans disbursed in 

U. S. dollars and/or foreign currencies 3/, where the principal obligations are denominated in U. S. dollars and are repayable in U. S. dollars, or in foreign currencies at the option of the U. S. Government. 

3/ Includes transfers of goods and services which create a principal obligation to be repaid over a period of years.

This (b) "Repayable in foreign currency with maintenance of dollar value. includes loans disbursed in U. S. dollars and/or foreign currencies 3/, where the principal obligations are denominated in U. S. dollars, but which are repayable in foreign currencies at the option of the borrowers at the exchange rate existing at the time of repayment. On these transactions, the 

United States does not assume an exchange risk and will be repaid in foreign currency in an amount equivalent to the stated dollar obligation. It should be noted, however, that the United States does not receive payment in dollars, but rather in foreign currencies, most of which cannot be freely converted into dollars. In many cases these foreign currencies are used to meet U. S. expenses in these countries, thereby reducing the amount of dollars which would otherwise be converted for such purposes. (c) "Repayable in foreign currency without maintenance of dollar value." 

This includes loans disbursed in U. S. dollars and/or foreign currencies 3/, where the principal obligations are denominated and repayable in foreign currencies. Also included are credits where the principal obligations are denominated in dollars and which are repayable in foreign currencies at a fixed, unvarying exchange rate. In these cases the U. S. Government assumes the exchange risk. (a) "Repayable in U. S. dollars equal to stated foreign currency amounts." 

This applies to one loan to the Industrial Development Bank of Israel which was disbursed in U. S. dollars, where the principal obligation is denominated in Israeli pounds and which is repayable in U. S. dollars at the exchange rate existing at the time of repayment. In this case, the United States bears the risk of devaluation of the foreign currency.

In all cases "maintenance of dollar value" applies only at the time of repayment of the principal obligations and payment of interest by the borrowing country. These countries have no obligation to maintain the dollar equivalent 

37 Includes transfers of goods and services which create a principal obligation to be repaid over a period of years.

of foreign currency balances held by the United States once payment has been made. Similarly, countries have no obligation to maintain the dollar value of repayments made in their currencies requested by the United States, at its option, on credits classified as "repayable in U. S. dollars." 

The classification by currency of repayment does not necessarily mean that current transactions take place in the stated currency, because of the many options that may prevail. One problem arising from such options is the determination of the applicable rate of interest for the installment of principal and interest being tendered. The rate shown in these tables is that which would be applicable if the repayment installment were to be tendered in the stated currency. (See reference symbols A, B, and D, above.) 

Calculations of Dollar Equivalents 

Because of the differing situations with regard to the exchange value and convertibility of foreign currencies, it is difficult to arrive at accurate comparability of foreign currency data as among various geographic areas, countries, programs, and currencies of repayment. The problem is only minor in respect of loans "repayable in foreign currency with maintenance of dollar value," for the obligations are denominated in dollars and all current activity is stated in dollar amounts. 

The more difficult problem arises in determining the U. S. dollar equivalent for data on loans "repayable in foreign currency without maintenance of dollar value," where activity is recorded in foreign currency amounts. Nevertheless, an attempt has been made to achieve some degree of comparability through the calculation of dollar equivalents for such transactions on the basis of procedures stipulated in regulations of the Department of the Treasury. Foreign currency data are shown for each credit and are identified by the reference symbol *. However, since dollar equivalent data are not presently available for each individual foreign currency loan, a 10

single dollar equivalent is shown for the total of all credits under a specified program in each country. The totals reported here frequently include the equivalent for "inactive" loans which have been completely disbursed and repaid, in those instances where the operating agency has not separately calculated the dollar equivalent of "inactive" and active credits. In the future it is expected that dollar equivalent data for individual transactions will be made available, so that this discrepancy can be eliminated. 

Amounts utilized (disbursements), principal collections, and interest payments are converted into U. S. dollar equivalents at the exchange rates prevailing for the period in which the transaction occurred, as required by Treasury Circular No. 930. 

All outstanding balances as well as the undisbursed balance (not explicitly shown in this report) of credits denominated in foreign currencies are converted to U. S. dollars at exchange rates prevailing as of the date of the report, also in accordance with Treasury Circular No. 930. When a foreign currency obligation is repaid in U. S. dollars at the option of the borrower, the repayment is recorded in foreign currency units for purposes of this report. Transactions of the Social Progress Trust Fund are valued as reported by the Inter-American Development Bank, which serves as administrator of the Fund. 

Split Loan Transactions 

There are some loans in which part is repayable in U. S. dollars and part in foreign currency. The following reference symbols are used to designate these loans: 11

*M This portion of the loan is repayable in U. S. dollars and another part is repayable in foreign currency with maintenance of dollar value. *N This portion of the loan is repayable in U. S. dollars and another part is repayable in foreign currency without maintenance of dollar value. ⭑$ This portion of the loan is repayable in foreign currency and another part is repayable in U. S. dollars. 

SUPPLEMENTAL INFORMATION 

Some credits extended by the Defense Department under the Foreign Assistance Act have been sold to the Export-Import Bank. The amount of such credits is added in the entry "Worldwide-Unspecified" for operations under the Export-Import Bank Act and deducted in the same entry for operations under the Foreign Assistance Act. 

Seeming discrepancies among utilizations (disbursements), repayments, and outstanding balances which appear in the data for loans in the "without maintenance of dollar value" category are a consequence of fluctuations in exchange rates.

Totals are the rounded sums of unrounded numbers, so that the rounded detail entries do not necessarily add to these totals. 

Additional reference symbols in the tables of this report indicate the following special conditions:

Y In the cases of China (Taiwan) and the Soviet Union, the entries under "War Account Settlements" are included in country and aggregate totals, although final settlements for World War II accounts with these countries have yet to be reached. 12

Q By agreement, the Republic of China is not at this time being called upon to make payments on portions of four loans made to the Republic of China prior to 1947 when the seat of government was on the mainland. These loans relate to assets no longer under control of the Republic of China (Taiwan). 

Collections 

A supplemental table has been included showing amounts overdue 90 days or more (as reported by the responsible agencies) as of the date of the report. A credit with an amount overdue is not necessarily in default. The arrearages reported are frequently technical, pending conclusion of discussions or negotiations between the borrower and the collecting agency. Footnotes following the table contain selected significant comments made by the collecting agencies in their reports submitted to the Department of the Treasury under the requirements of 

Treasury Circular No. 1080. 

Relationship of This Report to Other Published Data 

In addition to the present report, a summary table on "Outstanding 

Indebtedness of Foreign Countries on U. S. Government Credits" appears in the Annual Report of the Secretary of the Treasury on the State of the Finances (as of June 30) and in the Annual Report of the National Advisory 

Council on International Monetary and Financial Policies (as of December 31).

The data here given agree by country and program with those tables with a few minor exceptions arising from the timing of agency reports and the treatment of Commodity Credit Corporation (CCC) credits. The main body of this report includes CCC transactions extended since April 27, 1967 13

under GSM-4 regulations while CCC credits extended prior to April 27, 1967 under GSM-3 regulations are reported only in the footnote below 4/. 

Under GSM-4, CCC finances exports through the purchase of the exporters' accounts receivable which was not done under GSM-3. The Annual Report of the Secretary of the Treasury reports outstanding indebtedness under GSM-3 and GSM-4. 

Data Not Included in This Report This report does not include information on indebtedness due on any international claims settlements in which the U. S. Government has entered into an agreement with a foreign government in order to collect claims due to American nationals (except for such claims included in the statistics on the German World War I indebtedness). This information may be found in the Annual Report of the Secretary of the Treasury on the State of the Finances. Information on the value of foreign currencies held on deposit by the U. S Government which have been generated from the sale of agricultural and other commodities or services by the Government is not contained herein. This information is reported regularly in the 

Treasury Bulletin and in the Combined Statement of Receipts, Expenditures, and Balances of the United States Government, published by the Department of the Treasury. Also excluded are data on Commodity Credit Corporation (CCC) commercial exporter credits under GSM-3 regulations prior to April 27, 1967, 4/ and data on loans or other investments made by U. S. private institutions, even if such transactions carry credit risk insurance or any other guarantee by a U. S. Government agency. 4 Amounts outstanding for shipments to the countries listed as of the date of this report on CCC credits with original maturity of more than one year and extended before April 27, 1967: 871,540

Part II Foreign Debts Owed to International Organizations

Part II-Explanatory Note 

Part II of this report lists active individual credits authorized by international development-lending institutions to which the U.S. Government has made capital contributions. These data include loans and credits by the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development (IBRD), the Inter national Development Association (IDA), the Inter-American Development Bank 

(IDB), the Asian Development Bank (ADB), and loans and equity investments of the International Finance Corporation (IFC). Loans from the Social Progress 

Trust Fund, made by the Inter-American Development Bank as administrator on behalf of the United States, are included in Part I of this report as debts owed to the U.S. Government. The data in this part of the report have been compiled from the loan statements of the specified lending institutions, and are presented in a form comparable with Part I. 

Data presented by the IBRD and the IDA include, in addition to loans to member government, loans (a) to political subdivisions of member countries, and (b) to public or private enterprises in the territory of members with the member's guarantee. Any loan to a political subdivision or territory which has become independent since the loan was authorized, is classified as to country of the borrower, not as to guarantor, in these tables. 

EXPLANATION OF TABLES Date Authorized 

Entries in the "Data Authorized" column are those published by the lending agencies and vary somewhat in definition. Dates shown are those of the original action and do not reflect subsequent amendments. 

Amount Authorized 

Entries in the "Amount Authorized" column are shown net of cancellation, refunding, expiration, or termination of any part of the original commitment. 

Portions of a loan that have been sold or transferred to a participating lender are also deducted from this column. Entries in the "Status of Credit" columns also exclude activity on portions of credits sold or transferred. 131

The difference between the figure shown for "Amount Authorized" and the amount "Utilized" (or disbursed) represents the amount that, as of the date of the report, was available for further utilization. 

Interest Collected

The IBRD, the IDA, and the IFC do not publish data on "Interest Collected" on individual loans, and this information is therefore not given in the tables of Part II. 

The rate of return on IFC investments is particularly difficult to summarize statistically in the "Interest Rate" column, for the rate shown fails to take into account possible returns to the IFC from profit participation arrangements, from stock options, from equity conversion rights, or from similar contractual arrangements which may provide additional returns to the IFC. 

Contingent interest (i.e., over and above fixed interest) may also be included in the contracts, calling for a larger return to the Corporation should the profits of a project exceed original expectations. Furthermore, such contingent interest may be deferrable, noncancelable, or cumulative. Entries in the 'Interest Rate" column are shown as EQUITY for those IFC credits which are wholly equity investments. 

In the "Interest Rate" column, the following symbols are used for investments and loans with relatively complicated return provisions: 

Part of this investment is an equity investment; the rate shown is applicable to the loan portion. applicable X The interest rate on this loan was not fixed at the time of signature of the loan agreement. Interest will be applied to each portion of the loan at the institution's standard rate when it is withdrawn from the loan account. 

Other reference symbols used are explained in the Explanatory Note to Part I of this report. 132
